Japan has occupied one-ninth

telegram ‘sent by various public 
bodies in Hankow to’ Viscount Cecil 
-of Chelwood, head of the’ British 
delegation to the Conference of the 
International Peace Campaign 
which is opening in London tomor- 
row to discuss ways’ and means of 
‘boycottirig . Japan: and’ ‘helping 
China, ‘ ; 

The telegram urges the Con- 
ference to adopt the following re- 
solutions :— 

Firstly, “that Japan is the enemy 
‘of mankind; . 

Secondly, enforce economic. and 
financial sanctions against Japan 
and ban the export of munitions, 
technicians and foodstuffs. to 
Japan; . : 
_ Thirdty, “gender economie, finan- 
cial and technical assistance to 
‘China who should be supplied with 
munitions in order to assist her in 
her reaistance; 

Fourthly, make an appeal to the 
League of Nations. to” enforce 
articles XVI and XVI of the 
‘Covenant ‘and adopt a resolution 
refusing to recognise the bogus re- 

“gimes ‘established in China by| 
Japan.—(Reuter).

KING CAROL ISSUES" 
PROCLAMATION 


Bucharest, Feb, 11. 

A prochamation Issued by King 
Carol has dispelled all doubt re- 
garding the existence of a non- 
parliamentary dictatorship in Ru- 
‘mania, the King stating that 
though for eight years he had en- 
deavoured to rule the country on 


a parliamentary ' basis, the pro-'|. — 


paganda connected with the ‘elec- 


tion provoked a serious unrest in |’ 


the Life and spirit of the people. 
“Rumania must be saved,” he 
‘sald, “and I am resolved to’ put 
an end to the present’ dangerous 
‘conditions and without Hesitation 
pass urgent reforms for which I 
have formed a government from 
responsible men free from all party 
activity and who will issue decrees 
)in accordance with the new 
requirements of the country,” 
Martial law and o rigorous cen- 


‘|sorship now prevails throughout 


the country, Parllamentary and 
municipal elections, as well as 
other political activities Bale been 
suspended. . 

Troops ate on the move every- 
where with instructions to round 
up, all provocative agents whether 
of ‘the Right or Left parties and 
shoot to kill if necessary. 

Fascist and communist agents 
were on the’ eve of making Ru- 
mania a. second Spain, according 
;to political observers who dre con- 
vinced that Moscow chose the op- 
portune moment when Germany 
‘was occupled with internal trou- 
bles to effect the fall of the Goga 
Cabinet.— (Reuter).

A Papal Anniversary 


‘Today ts the sixteenth anniver- 
sary of the coronation of the 
Pope. It is being celebrated In the 
Catholic Cathedral by a. special 
thanksgiving service .at 5.30 p.m. 
when the Te Deum, the Church’s 
hymn oot thanksgiving, will be 
“ sung at Benediction of the Blessed 
Sacrament. The coronation of a 
néw Pope takes place a few days 
after his election and it was on 
last sunday, February. 6th, that 
the anniversary of the election of 
‘Pope Pius XI occurred.. The pre- 
sent Pope is the 260th of the Ine 
that begins, with St. Peter. In 
‘Rome on this day the traditional 
diplomatic greetings to the Sover- 
etgn Pontiff take: place, There 1s 
a special warmth about these 
meetings at the 'Vatican because 
of the personal popularity of "the 
present Pope with the: diplomatic 
representaives of foreign states. 
Greetings ore also offered to the 
Pope by the ‘Cardinals, who are 
‘hig deputies In the important de- 
partments of church government. 
Most of. the present members of) 
‘the College of Cardinals were ap- 
pointed by Pius XT, as a consider- 
able’ number of | the previous 
Cardinals died with the, past six- 
~ teen, years. m 


 PHE LATERAN TREATY. 
Another anniversary closely con- 
nected with Pope Pius XI 
also occurred in the: course: 
of . the - present week, the 
ruinth anniversary of the’ Lateran 
‘Treaty between the Pontiff and 
the Italian Government. This 
treaty was welcomed , by the 
Chureh no less than by the Ttallan 
State, for !t put an-end to the) 
‘thorny “Roman Question” which 
for a long time seemed practically 
insoluble. The Papal “| States 
through the grant of territory to 
the Church by the Holy Roman 
Empire and various Italian’ 
States, and this’ territory was 
finally seized by the State when 
‘the unification of Italy took place 
“In 1870. It was the constant claim 
of ‘the Pope that ‘his office as’ 
spiritual ruler of over three hund- 
red million people of all nationall. 
ties demanded that he should not 
be the subject of a foreign power, 
and while many Italian statesman 
realised the justice of this claim 
they did not see any means of 
_ satisfying ft In the actual cireum- 
stances existing in Italy, .The 
way; that was found in the ‘end: 
by recognising the Vatican as 4, 
tiny but independent state, seems 
ridiculously easy” when - lt was 
adopted, and it Has ‘proved a 
thoroughly satisfactory way, Many 
veople said that the papacy lost 
much of {ts romantic character 
when the Pope was no longer the 
«“protsoner of. the. Vatican,” but 
the Pope did ‘not want, romance 
but only freedom to, carry out his 
. duties as-a spiritual ruler. 
THE CATHOLIC ‘PRESS’ 
EXHIBITION 
The Exhibition of Catholic pert- 
odicals and pamphicts which ‘was 
held in St. Patrick's Club last 
Sunday under the auspices of the 
Catholic Truth Society of. Hong 
Kong proved a ‘very attractive 
display, and brought a constant 
stream, of visitors" throughout ‘the 
day; Several hundred people at- 
tended and 2 large number enter- 
ed their names as subscribers to 
‘one ‘or another of the papers and 
. magazines which were on -display. 
Most of the periodicals which 
mere chosen for particular atten- 
tion were either English or Chin- 
ese. As the number of weekly 
and. monthly Catholic periodicals 
jn English is very large only a 
comparatively small number were 
selected,- and -those "which were 
put on exhibition were those 
which were, deemed most useful 
or interesting. considering . the 
special position of the. people of 
"| ‘Hong Kong. Many who. visited 
the Exhibition were ‘surprised to 
see the large number of Catholic 
. periodicals published in Chinese, 
though in-reallty the number was 
greatly reduced owing to war con- 
ditions. Many Catholic periodicals 
published in the areas which are 
- now thescene of war have’ neces- 
_ sarily had to suspend publication.

WOUNDED 


Te CE a 


tional Christian Service Council 
for Wounded Soldiers in Transit 
’ is a purely independent and.volun- 
tary. organization sponsored by 
the Natlonal Christian Council of 
China, to ameliorate the suffering 
“of wounded soldiers being trans- 
ported from the ambulance col- 
lecting stations behind the firing 
lines to base hospitals, declared 
an official of the council. states 
the Ceutral China Post, 


- “Owing to disrupted commun- 
. ‘Teations,” he explained, “the time 
' -sequired. for travelling sometimes 

‘-gmounts to 10, 15 or even 20 days. 

‘This organization shall try to 
provide, beddings, clothes, food, 
_water or other forms of: comforts 
for the wounded: soldiers where 
‘and when they. dre most urgently 
in need of these things.” 
‘This 1s felt to be a necessary 
piece of work, he asserted, in splte 
‘of what the. government has 
already been doing. In. view of 
‘the war operations and the dis- 
‘pupted, communications, In his 
' opinion, the Army Medical service 
“Corps is in many ways handicap- 

ped in its actual.service to the 
“wounded soldiers, 

In ‘its work,’ the council” co. 
operates closely with the Interna- 
‘tlonal, Red Cross, the War Area 

. Service Corps, and the Army Me- 
-dical Service Corps,,. the official 
said. ; 

, ‘00- ORDINATION AGENCY 


The. aew, organization: “was also 
found, necessary, he ppidresded, 5 


we 


” 


various local .service groups and 
the Army Medical Service Corps. 
In most of the larger cities and 
towns there are many voluntary 
service groups which endeavour 
to do something for wounded 
soldiers, But, very often on account 
of “milltary reasons; they Army 
Medical Service Corps is unable} 
to extend facilities for relief work 
to such local groups. 


. Again, continued the official, At 


was felt tnat the council may help 
eliminate unnecessary duplication 
of efforts and. distribute more 
evenly the services of the mahy 
local service groups, There may be 
half a dozen voluntary. groups, 
especially in big cities, all doing 
the same thing at. the same place, 
he. explained, while in’. smaller} 
elties there: may, be. none at all. 
The present organization, .belng 
nation-wide, he stated,’ will survey 
the néeds as a whole and distri-’ 
bute service more evenly than’ it 
has ‘been possible before. 


‘The ‘act{vitles of the’ ‘council, 
according to the official, will at 
first be confined to the northern 
part of the ‘country, along the 
Lung-Hal Railway, with Chengéhow 
as the centre. The general head- 
quarters of the council are located

WELLS FARGO 


Painted on an heroic ‘canvas 


‘| with bold and colorfal strokes of | 


an inspired brush dipped into the | 
fountain heads of history itself, 
“Wells Fargo” is beautiful, inspir- 
bed romance—an important pic- 

botM in motion pictuure his- 
ae and as a tribute and reminder 
of America’s humble beginnings. 
{t.Js one of those’ woefully few 
pictures which {s entertainment 
for every member of the family, 
and 1t Is one of those pictures 
which everybody should se¢—at 
ue ance. 


{th Joel seeped Frances Dee 
a Bob Burns in the leading 


roles, “Wells Fargo” 1s a cavalcade 
of American history from the dis- 
[covery of gold in California untii 
the end of ‘the War Between the 
It treats its subject : 


States, 
estly, intelligently and symipathe- 
tically. Here is no sentimental 
clap-trap of the “hooray for our 
side” variety, which so many pro- 
ducers are unable to resist In 
making a picture from history. but 
a llving and breathing monument 
such as only the magic of the mo- 
tion picture can call into being.

DIVISIONS | 


A Drastié. eee 


’ "Reduction . 
The wisdom of the Suropean 


handy type of division has now 
‘been recognized {na most striking 
way by the United States army, 
writes the Times Milltary Corres- 
pondent. 

It‘ts officially proposed to. reduce 
the division to barely half its 
present manpower, and a remodell- 
ed division of this scale is un- 
dergoing service test. During the 
War most of the aymies'reorganiz- 
ed their divisions on, a nine- 


-]battallon basis, and though: the 


change -was Influenced by scarcity 
of men an appreciation of its 
milltary advantages made it per- 
manent, in general, after the War. 
Our own army was an exception 
in reverting to the 12-battalion 
division, But the American divi- 
sions were larger: still, and were 
not even reduced when they took 
part “in “the campaign on. the 
Western . Front, though thelr 
unwieldiness proved 2 handicap. 

The radical change now intended 
{s the immediate result of the re- 
port ofa War Department Board 
on the modernization of the 
Organization of the Army. “ 

The’ hewndiviston ts divided nto 
a command, a reconnalssance, "a 
combat,’ and a service echelon. 
The infantry part of it comprises 
three regiments, each of three 
Trifle battalions and one machine- 
gun battalion. The size of the rifle 
battalions has been much -reduced 
—to only 24 officers and 580: men 
aplece, "divided ‘into four’ com- 
panies, The machine-gun battalion 
is smaller still, consisting ‘of 22 
officers and 462 men, 

In ‘the new infantry organiza- 
tlon the brigade and the section 
have been abolished, thus simpl!- 


4dftying and quickening the. execu- 


tlon of orders; One brigadier- 
general is retained. to command 
the service echelon, and another 
os infantry adviser, inspector of 
training, and general assistant to 
the divisional commander. 


‘ARTILIERY COMPONENT 


. The ‘artillery component of the 
division consists of a fleld: artillery 
Tegiment ‘of 12 _,hatterles—six of 
75 mm. howitzers: three of 81 mm. 
mortars, and three of 105 “mm. 
howitzers.’ ‘The . "reconnalssance 
echelon consists of & squadror of 
motorized cavalry. : 

The effective weapon-power of 
the division, consists of ‘3,998 sem!- 
324, ght ma- 
“Mne-guns,. 72 machine-guns of 

0 calibre, 36 of .50 calibre. 36 
infantry light mortars, 36 artillery 
81 mm. mortars, 24 75 mm. howlt- 
ters, and 12.105 mm, howitzers, 
Yet the "scale of man-power 4s 
Teduced to 673 officers and 12,839 
men, ° ‘ 

Even this Is considered too"large 
by Major-General Fox Conner. 
who was an official observer for 
the War Department at the pre- 
liminary tests of the new organiza- 
tions last autumn. He has expres- 
sed the definite’ opinion that it 
should nuriber no more than 10,- 
000 officers and men, and hinted 
that the War Department Js likely 
to’ reach this declelon:

WAR DAMAGE 
‘IN CHINA 


> —- 

The following letter was publish 
ed in the Times. 

Bir--With reference 
letter of Sir. Neill - Malcolm 
in The Times of January 4 
headed “War Damage in China,” 
I would: Ike strongly; to up- 
hold his opinion that, though it 
is not to be expected that educa- 
tional estblishments should escape 
military attention, it would appear 
that they have been regarded’ as 
especially desirable targets, 

As a volunteer and.an eye- 
witness of the aerial and: artillery 
bombardment of, Nankai University 
at Tlentsin, it seemed very obvious 
to' me that the reason could not 
have been to drive cut Chinese 
troops, as was claimed in the 
Japanese statement: on January 21, 
since at that stage there were no. 
Chinese troops in the vicinity. — 

It may be true, as then stated. 
that the University was a centre of 
strong anti-Japanese. feeling, as 
was the case among most students 
in China, but since it was the 
summer vacation the bulidings 
were empty. Therefore the heavy 


9 "the 


bombardment appeared only to], 


have the object of destroying a 
valuable seat of learning..- 
Furthermore, the next day I saw: 
Japanese soldiers arrive in lorries, 
with tins of petru), and deliberately 


fset Nght to the remains of the 
totally , 


partially shattered and 

deserted college, buildings. 

" Yours’ very truly, 

i ‘" JOBN 8. DAVIS.

Surrey, 


“ONE-WAY STREETS” FOR 
Py os _ AIRCRAFT. 


‘Because ‘of the great increase in, 
aerial traffic the Air Ministry has 
decidea to parcel the sky in bad 
weather into a series of “one-way 
streets.” This scheme which came 
{nto turce on February 1, is known 
as the, “directional scheme.” All 
alrerult flyjng im cloud or bad 


. {visibility at any particular height 


will have to: fly on approximately 
the same course. 

The system will apply by day 
and by night ever the- whole ef 
Great, Britain and Northern Ire- 
land to aircraft, flying'at between, 
3,300 feet and 18,600 feet,.with the 
exception of an area in the Chan- 
ngl and South-east England. and 
another, covering | ‘part of North- 
west’ England and. ther Trish: Sea. 
‘FOUR ZONES © 


“tne airspace between 3,300. and 
18,600 feet above sea-level ts divid-: 
ed into four horizéntal zones, 3,300 
tu 6,600 feet, 7,000 to- 10,600 feet, 
11,000 to-14,€00 fest, 11,000 to 14, 
6U0 feet, and 15,000 to 18,600: feet. 
Each of. these zones 1s subdivided 
jnita layers of 300 feet, and each of 
these layers 1s’ allotted to courses 
lying within a thirty degree ‘sector 
of the compass. : 

“Tt. 1s. appreciated,” says. “the 
notiee to airmen anouncing ‘the 
system, “that -It ts impracticable 
to..maintaln precise heights ‘with 
accuracy, but the point of primary 


ene id seat aircratt should 


\be, flown “within the. .appropriate 
layer of 300 feet, All ascents and 
descents 6n change of zone or layer { 
and on entering the system. should 
be made as quickly as possible. 

All aircraft should carry a 
course-hefght. indicator, for the 
directional system sarcraae from 
® cord.

BOMB ‘EXPLODES OUTSIDE THREE, "LIVES MIGHT BE JAPAN'S TROOPS' r 
_ NEWS OFFICES . 


“4 Hole, Torn Through Wall 


A hand-grenade. es on the 
first floor of the Shanghal Even- 
ing Post and Mercury dullding 
In Avenue Edward VII early on a 
morning tore a ‘hold 
through one wall and managed to 
damage no less than three offices, 
says the North China Dally News, 


The hand-grenade, of the “potato- 
variety, was thrown at 
abaut 5 o'clock and smashed a hole 
through the wall of’ the United 
(American news agency) 
office, wrecked the door leadinz 
Into the office of. Mr. H. G. W. 
Woodhead, editor, of Oriental 
Affairs, and damaged furniture 
and broke some glass in the office 
of the editor of the Shanghai 
Evening Post and Mercury. 


‘Que to the fact that the premises 


-|were almost deserted 4t the time. 


no'.one was injured. A coolle 
sleeping on a bench near where 
the bomb was thrown. however. 
had a severe fright when the bomb 
exploded and tore its wav through 
the wall‘only six inches .or so’ from 
his feet. ‘ 


The bomb-thrower. who ts said 
to have been dressed in, a long 
Chinese’ gown,‘ calmly made his 
escape immediately after throwing 
the missile, He was actually seen 
by twa Chinese coolles In the hav 


‘|but as they believed he was armed. 


they made no attempt to ston 
him, Chinese in the street below. 
asilwell as the Sikh watchman on 


the ground, floor, did not see the 
mian come out of the butiding. 


When the Frendh Police arrived. 
however, it was obvious that: the 
man was no longer on the pre- 
mises. The-United Press office was

SAVED 


pF 
Prolonged Respiesttal 
For Electric Shock 


Hundreds of lives might be saved 
annually if persons electrically 
shocked and apparently dead, were 
given prolonged artificial respira- 
ton, 

This conclusion has been reached 
ug the result of studies carried out 
nder the auspices of the Harvard 


"Medical School and the Boston City 


Hospital by Dr. Leo Alexander, 

No person, it is declared, should 
be pronounced dead, however badly 
shocked, until twelve hours uf artl- 
ficlal respiration had been tried or 
{ntl rigor mortis had set in. 

Official United States census fig- 
ures show that in the five years 
1930—1934 nearly 4,000 persons 
lost thelr lives from electrical ac- 
cidents. In 1934 ond 1935, eight 
hundred lost their lives from light- 
ning alone, 

Both forms of shock should re- 


celve the same treatment, the stud- | cleared, he saild,.were areas which. 


had been destroyed by fire and | ¥ 


Jes revealed. 

It was sald that in groups of 
workers who are well trained in 
the “prone pressure” method of ar- 
tificlal respiration,and who kept it 
up for long periods the recovery, 
rate, {s 67 per cent. 

Where the respiration was begun 
within three minutes after the 
shock, 70 per centirecovered, After 
four minutes, the recovery rate w2s 
$8 per cent.

MALAYAN 


Tin’ Controt’ Shine: 2 

With the international tin cont rol dineme ‘working, it. not to per-: 
fection, at least as satisfactorily as itis possible for any acheme to 
work when its primary object'ls the stablisation of price controlled by 
the regulation of supply, it 1s not unnatural that there should be a 
strong body of opinion which subscribes to the view that well, showld 
be left allone, says the Malaya Tribune. While there Js great deal to- 
be satd for that point of view, it’ is scarcely logical to regard it as” 
final and ruling out any possibility of improvement in the scheme. 
-‘That'there is room for improvement is freely admitted by the Malayan 
tin industry which has carried the heavy end of the. control burden. 
ever since its inception.. The dangers attendant on swopping horses 
while crossing a stream are well-known, but occasions arise when the 
operation hecomes necessary. i 


Cut’ In’ Quota 


. The recent cut in the quota was made for the purpose of check- 
ing’the alarming decline in: price due to what some people have been: 
pleased to term the tride “recession” in America. Plain-speaking call- 
ed ita slump, but that is beside the polnt, ‘The price did not responed to 
the quota cut in the manner antlelpated, which Serves to demonstrate 
that such action ts not ulways rellable in effecting the regulation of 
the market. To boll ‘the whole thing down,: market regulation, in” 
the last resort, resolves. itself on a question of price. The proposil 
has been made elsewhere that the International Tin Committee should 
fix a basic price above which production ‘would be unrestricted and 
below which it would be drastically curtulled. Tt is n suggested that: 
the adoption of this proposal would solve the’ ‘prob lems of the. tin 
industry, but as the LTC. ts in’ ‘complete ‘control’ with the object of’ - 
maintaining the tdustry on as prosperous a plane as possible, it 
should at least give serious consideration to this and any other sug- 
gestion which carrles the Ting of practicability about {t.

Verviers (Belgium), Feb. 11. 
At ‘the Belgian frontier posts the 
gendarmes have been doubled after 
reinforcement of ; the German 
police on the German side of the 
frontier.—(Reuter), 

It is understood the object of 
strengthening the German police 
was to prevent the departure from 
Germany of any persons whose 
‘papers were not in order, \A ¢ cer- 
tain amount of effervescence pre- 
yalls in some German cities, dc- 
cording to Teports received here.— 
(Reuter).

The Austro-German, 
been virtually: closed ‘by the Ger- 
man. authorities since yesterday 
afternoon. 

‘Tt ds learned the; “porder between 
| Germany ‘and ‘Upper Austria has 
been completely closed, and on the 
Tyrol frontier normal trafitc -has 
been stopped, but a few persons 
were allowed to pass under strict, 
observation—(Reuter). a 

NO FEAR OF REVOLT 

: ‘Berlin, Feb. 11. 

Nazi circles do not expect any- 
thing in the’ nature of a revolt or 
unrest, although it 1s admitted that. 
there fs some restiveness of old 
army officers, especially {p such 
districts as Pomerania or East 
Prussia, where they have been 
brought up in the tradition of the 
old army; but, Nazis deny that 
trouble {s likely. 

It is gathered, however, that a 
certain amount of weeding out of 
officers will occur in the course of 
time. These are the officers who 
are not sympathetic with the new 
regime. Itis polnted out that the 
army was built tip most hurriedly 
and many officers brought back 
who were over steeped in the old 
traditions. (Reuter), =“

Extensive plans for the ‘relief of

Cina’ war have been completed

jion yen. ’

and men will be given financial 
assistance for entering engineer-

ment of a new Rumanian Governnient is greeted with sratifica- 

. thon in political quarters since the Gora Cabinet did not enjoy 

any sympathies here. Comments in this morning's papers contain 

merely brief teferences to the Jewish question, and to’ the Rama- 
nian King’s decision to assume the “role of dictator.” 


The papers, howevér, express the view that the recent re- | 
orientation of the Rumanian policy, which aimed at loosening 
Rumanio’s ties to France, and strengthening her relations with 
Italy and Germany, will be clncertupted by the fall of the Goga 


’ Cabinet. 


“Le Jour” believes that the new Ramanian Government will 
‘endeavour to improve Romanian relations with" France and. Bri-+ 
tain since Rumania’s critical situation is making the assistance 
of the great Western Powers imperative.—(Transocean).

te ‘break through the town’ 's defences, but yach time were driven back 
‘by heavy artillery fire.. The Japanese were at .one tine” in a tight 
corner, but the timely arrival of reinforcements, together with planes,. 
‘which bombed the Chinese positions, saved a difficult situation,

during the fighting.

: Shanghal, Feb, 10. 

‘Asked by pressmen,: today, how 
many Japanese soldiers had re- 
cently,been punished for breaches 
ot discipline, Mr. Hidaka, Coun- 
sellor of the Japanese Embassy, 
Teluctant to commit himself, but. 
Pressed further, admitted that the 
number was more than ten. 

However, he declined to disclose 
the charges: against’ the accused 
or the sentences imposed. : 

Mr. Hidaka said the conditions 
at Nanking had greatly tmproved 
since the New Year and declared 
that the number of refugees in"the | 
‘safety ‘zone had been reduced | 


from 240,000 to "103,000. 


Mr. Hidaka added that refugees 
had returned ‘to thelr homes 
in the five. districts ‘of Nanking 
into which the Chinese autoho- 
mous commission, had divided the 
city.—(Reuter), |

of Commerce and Industry and 
other major economic organtsa-

brary In New, York next April, in

Japan,” Foreign Office officials 
revealed today. | , 

Similar Ubrartes will be estab-

world later. The announcement 
followed close on the heels of a 
letter from Mr."Yusuke Tsurumi,

now in New York as “Goodwill 
Envay.” urging the establishment 
of reference Ubrarles abroad,

Cultural Relations and -other cul- 
tural bodies. —(Reiter)

SOVIET EXPELS 68 
ITALIAINS 
epee 

__ Milan, Feb. 10, 

Sixty-eight Itallan nationals 
who: had been expelled ‘from the 
Soviet Union arrived in Venice on 
Thursday on, board the steamer 
{Vista” a 

‘It Js declared that those ex- 
pelled were labourers and’ crafts- 
men from Batum, Tiflls and 
Odessa, whose parents and grand- 
narents had. migrated from Italy 
two or even three - generations 
back, but, because the descendants 
steadfastly refysed to adopt Rus- 
sian citizenship, they were re- 
garded as undesirable by the .Bo- 
viet Government and finally ex- 
pelled from the country, without 


being “allowed to take more than a 


mere necessities with them. 
These people will be provided 
with homes in the districts from 


which their parents migrated.— 
(Trans-Ocean)

KING’S VISIT 
TO INDIA 
' POSTPONED. 


Burden On Revenues 
_ Mentioned 


London, Feb. 10. 

. King Ueorge wil not visit India 
this year, An official communique 
status the mutter Js of deep regret 
to the King that he has once more 
felt obliged to postpone the visit, 
bUt his Majesty feels that, after 
careful examination of the matter’ 
in ali Its aspects, both nancial and 
general, he would not be’ justified, 
in {mposing any additional burden’ 
on the existing revenues of India 
at a time when the call on them in 
connection with the tnanguration 
of provincial autonomy, Is already 
heuvy. . 

Moreover, his Majesty would de- 
Plore loss or inconvenience result- 
ing from any change of plans that 
might prove necessary at a later 
date, 

With these considerations in 
mind, his Majesty ha’ reluctantly 
come tu the” conclusion that it, 
Would be advisable to walt until 
general world , outlook becomes 
more settled and the financial as- 
Pects more definite, before oom- 
mitting himself to an engagement 
so long in advance. 

In communicating his decision to 


Lord Zetland, Hls Majesty once |' 


More repeated his strong desire to 


visit India and his intention of |’ 


doing so as soon as Circumstances 
permit. — (Reuter) .

RUMANIAN 
GOVERNMENT 
RESIGNS ° 


Replaced By ° 


Coalition? 

j Bucharest, ‘Feb. 11. 

A series of sudden Internal polt- 
tical developments led to the re- 
signation of the Right Wing Gov- 
ernment of M. Octavian Gaga, on 
Thursday. evening, 

The action came unexpectedly, 
So that the Press and the general | 
public were taken by surprise, Al- 
though nothing, except the bare 


fact of the announcement has béen |. J" 
announced officially, it 1s known |. 


that King Carol received the ‘Pre- 
sident of the Llberal Party, M, 
Dinu Bratianu, and on Thursday 
a number of other party leaders’ 
were summoned to special audien- 
ces at the Royal Palace. " 

The events which 


strictly secret, but by’ late after- 
noon rumours had beguh to cir- 
culate that the Left Wing opposi- 
tion: was - endeavouring to bring 
about the fall of the Gaga Govern- 
ment and to replace it with. a 
coalition group. 

During the early evening hours 
@ council of ministers was called, 
following which, M. Gaga visited’ 
the King. - Shortly before seven 
o'clock it was officially announcer 
that the Government had handed 
in its resignation—(Trans-Ocean), 


ITALIAN PLANE CLASH 


« " Parts, Feb. 10. 

The Italian plane which was en 
route to London.to make an at- 
tempt to break the London-Cape 
Town record, crashed while flying 
over the Le Bourget air fleld here 
on Thursday and: was completely 
destroyed. 

The crew of the machine, which 
was piloted by the prominent 
Itlian airman, Signor, Albertine, 
escaped uninjured. 
Further details of the crash have 
not yet been published. —(Traris- 
Ocean). 7

disappointing. * Borrachito inay yet 
emulate his stable companion 
Gordlto, the champion sub. of 1937,

AMONGST THE AUSTRALIANS 
With constant attendance on 


gallop mornings the opinion seems 
[more than. ever confirmed that 


at the most, the Australlan’ ponies 
of his season are an extremely 
disappointing batch. Fast gallops 
have been the exception rather 
than the ‘rule and of. those that |: 
can be placed In the former cate- 
gory Little Audrey {s about the 
only one that finishes well. Criffel 
and Murray River have both cover- 
the ‘mile inside two minutes this 
last week but were definitely tired 
at'the finish. King’s Privilege with 
‘|Mr, Needa up was a trifle slower 
last Sunday, taking 2,00-2/5" yet 
the pony finished much stronger 
and ts superior to the two afore- 
{méntloned, My selection of ' the 
‘\best ones are: Little Audrey, 
King’s Privilege,. Bredon, Criffel, 


fortunately, two promising en- 
trants. A Lovely Time and An- 
nabella, are .on the walking list 
and have stopped training. 

‘My next article will appear on 
Friday next when I will endeavour 
to analyze the probable starters in 
the various events on the opening 
|day of the mecting .and comment 

on thelr chances. On Saturday 
morning, February 19, my final 
selections for, the first Day will be 
published.

Lal Wing, 28, appeared before

on ‘two ‘counts of larcény ot

appeared for. the defendant and 
Inspector W, A. Russell prosecuted.

dunt; with two others, came up to 
the complainant In Chatham Road 
on Wednesday night, and, on the 
pretext of searching for heroin 
pills they held up the complainant, 
| Two of the men-made off with $2.30 
and the complainant. caught hold 
ofthe defendant. 

After evidence had. been “given, '| : 
defendant was discharged on one 
count, afd hearing on the other 

| [was adjourned to February. 22.

Ordinary air mail letters for Imperial Alrways Direct. Ser- 
vice to Europe etc, will, until further notice, be closed at Kow- 
loon Post Office’and General Post Office 9.00 a.m. on Sundays, 
Letters for this Service may be posted in the ordinary posting 
boxes at Kowloon: Post Office and General Post Office. They 

should be clearly marked “By Air Mail” and bear sufficlent. post- 
age. Insufficlently prepaid letters may “be taxed with double the 
deficiency or forwarded by Steamer Service, at the discretion of 
the Post, Office,
